People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello Dr. Smith. I... I came in today because things have just been so unsettling lately, and I really didn't know what else to do. I live by myself in this little studio apartment, you know, just a small place, and usually it's very peaceful when I get off work. I'm a personal care aide, so I spend all day on my feet taking care of other folks, and when I get home, I just want quiet. 

But I keep hearing these voices. And I don't mean like the TV is on next door or folks talking out on the street, I mean it sounds as clear as somebody standing right next to me in my little studio. They just keep saying my name, over and over, and telling me that my work is being judged. It's so real, I swear someone is standing right behind me watching me, telling me I'm being judged for what I do and how I'm doing it. But then I turn around to look, and the house is completely quiet. There is absolutely nobody there. I check the locks, I look in the bathroom, I check the closets, and it's just empty, but I still hear them talking to me like they're right in the middle of the room with me. It just doesn't stop, and I... I just can't figure out how they're getting in or where it's coming from when it's just me in there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, Lord, it feels like forever, to be completely honest with you. But... if I really sit down and think back on it, count the weeks and everything, it’s been about nine months now. 

I remember because I thought at first it was just me being worn out. You know how it is when you're working so hard and your brain just kind of plays tricks on you? I'm on my feet all day, lifting folks, bathing them, and I'd come home and be washing my dishes or folding my scrubs for the next day, and I'd hear it. Just that whisper. And I'd tell myself, you just need a good night's sleep, you're just exhausted from the shift. But then it didn't stop after a good night's sleep. It just kept going and going, every single day, and it got louder. 

And then that feeling started, that awful, heavy feeling that they aren't just saying my name, they are watching every single thing I do. Nine whole months of coming home, locking my deadbolt, checking the windows, and still feeling like eyes are just burning right into the back of my neck. These fixed fears that they're analyzing my work, judging if I turned my patients right or if I spoke to them properly... it's been nine months of this constant, terrifying feeling that I'm being watched and graded by whoever is standing right there in the room with me. It's just exhausting, doctor, it really is, because you can't ever truly sit down and rest when you've had that going on in your own home for nine months straight.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, as far as knowing where I am or what the date is, I'm fine on that front. I know exactly what day it is and I know I'm right here in your office. But my focus... Lord, my focus is just completely shot to pieces lately. It's like my brain just won't stay put on any one thing for more than a single second. 

I'll sit down at my little kitchen table—it's this bright yellow table I picked up at a yard sale a few years back, I thought the color would cheer up the apartment, you know—and I’ll try to finish a care note for the agency. Because we have to write down everything, what the client ate, if they took their pills, all those details they need for the records. Or, if I'm off the clock, I'll try to just sit and do a crochet row. I'm trying to make a little baby blanket for my niece, just a simple pattern. But I just can't stick to it. I'll have the pen in my hand or the yarn looped over my finger, and then... I don't know, a car door will slam outside on the street, and suddenly I'm up out of my chair looking out the glass to see who's parking. 

And then maybe there's a bird at the window, just a little sparrow or something picking at the sill, and I'm just standing there watching that bird instead of doing my work. Or I'll turn back around and just notice that bright yellow color on the table, and it just pulls me right away. Next thing I know, I’m off doing something else before I even know it. I'll be in the bathroom scrubbing the sink because I saw a yellow sponge, completely forgetting that I was supposed to be writing down Mrs. Miller's blood pressure or finishing that row of yarn. It's just exhausting, doctor, because I start a hundred different things and my mind just gets yanked away by every little noise outside or every color in the room. I just bounce around my little apartment like a pinball all evening and nothing ever actually gets finished.

Oh, my mood... well, yes, it's just been all over the place, but not so much the hopeless kind. It’s more like... I’ll go from feeling so bright and chosen, like God Himself has tapped me on the shoulder for something truly grand. But then, almost out of nowhere, that same energy just turns... sour. It gets prickly and hot. I'll go right from feeling like I'm on top of the world to snapping hard at someone over a tiny, insignificant little thing. Like the other day at work, I was helping one of my regular clients, and he just didn't hear me right. He just asked me to repeat myself about what we were having for lunch. Just a simple, harmless little question, right? And I just snapped at him. I mean, I bit his head off over absolutely nothing, telling him I don't have time to say things twice. I felt awful about it later, because that's not me, I'm a patient person, I have to be for this job. You can't be mean to folks who need your help. But in that moment, my skin was just crawling with irritation. It’s just this wild swing from feeling like the most special, capable woman in all of Greenville to being so mean and irritable over a simple question, and I just can't seem to stop the train once it gets going like that.

I just bounce around my little apartment like a pinball all evening and nothing ever actually gets finished.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant changes in your mood, such as feeling unusually low or hopeless, or perhaps feeling extremely energetic and unable to sleep?\n\nPatient: Oh, my mood... well, yes, it's just been all over the place, but not so much the hopeless kind. I get this buzzing energy in my chest where I just know that Greenville can’t hold all my plans. I'll start thinking about how I'm going to reform the whole caregiving system, how I'm going to open a dozen facilities, and I just feel so big and important, like I could walk right through walls if I wanted to. My mind is just racing with all these magnificent ideas for the future, and I feel so good, so incredibly powerful and ready to take on the whole world. \n\nBut then, almost out of nowhere, that same energy just turns... sour. It gets prickly and hot. I'll go right from feeling like I'm on top of the world to snapping hard at someone over a tiny, insignificant little thing. Like the other day at work, I was helping one of my regular clients, and he just didn't hear me right. He just asked me to repeat myself about what we were having for lunch. Just a simple, harmless little question, right? And I just snapped at him. I mean, I bit his head off over absolutely nothing, telling him I don't have time to say things twice. I felt awful about it later, because that's not me, I'm a patient person, I have to be for this job. You can't be mean to folks who need your help. But in that moment, my skin was just crawling with irritation. It’s just this wild swing from feeling like the most special, capable woman in all of Greenville to being so mean and irritable over a simple question, and I just can't seem to stop the train once it gets going like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you have these periods of feeling powerful and grand, or when you feel irritable and mean, do the voices and the feeling of being watched happen at the same time, or do they also happen when your mood is completely normal?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's the thing that really gets me tangled up when I try to make sense of it all. Because, yes, when I'm buzzing high and feeling like I'm going to build that whole caregiving empire, they're there. And when I'm irritable and snapping at folks, they're there. But... it's not just then. 

I remember this one stretch, it was maybe a couple months ago. That big, grand energy just kind of fizzled out, like a balloon losing its air, and I was finally getting some proper rest. I was sleeping through the night, getting my solid eight hours, waking up feeling physically normal, you know? My mood wasn't flying high, and I wasn't snapping at anybody. I was just... going to work, coming home, feeling regular. 

But those voices... Lord, they didn't take a break just because my mood did. They stayed right there. I'd be sleeping fine, feeling calm, and I'd still come home to that whisper. And that awful, heavy feeling that they were in the room, watching me, tracking every little thing I did at work, judging if I rolled a patient right or charted my hours correctly... that kept going on for weeks. Just weeks and weeks of feeling completely normal in my mood, but still having to check the deadbolt and look over my shoulder because I knew they were there, grading me. So, no, they don't just happen when I'm feeling all powerful or mean. They stick around even when I'm just trying to be plain old me.

Well, let me think back on that to give you a proper number, because I want to be accurate. I remember exactly when it was, because it was right around the time I picked up a new morning shift with this sweet older gentleman over on Elm Street. I'd say it was at least three or four solid weeks in a row.

And I had this heavy, terrible feeling sitting right on my shoulders that the agency had sent folks to watch me from outside the window. I just knew they were tracking my hours, judging every little stir of that spoon, grading whether I was a good enough caregiver. It's just so exhausting, doctor, because you'd think if you're finally sleeping good and your mood is quiet, the rest of it would just pack up and go away too, right? But no, for those several weeks, day in and day out, I'd go to work feeling completely regular in my spirits, but I was still looking over my shoulder, checking the blinds, just waiting for the people who were grading me to show themselves. It just dragged on and on like that for nearly a month before things shifted again...

I'm a personal care aide, so I spend all day on my feet taking care of other folks, and when I get home, I just want quiet. \n\nBut I keep hearing these voices. And I don't mean like the TV is on next door or folks talking out on the street, I mean it sounds as clear as somebody standing right next to me in my little studio. But then I turn around to look, and the house is completely quiet. There is absolutely nobody there. But... if I really sit down and think back on it, count the weeks and everything, it’s been about nine months now. \n\nI remember because I thought at first it was just me being worn out. I'm on my feet all day, lifting folks, bathing them, and I'd come home and be washing my dishes or folding my scrubs for the next day, and I'd hear it. Just that whisper. And I'd tell myself, you just need a good night's sleep, you're just exhausted from the shift. But then it didn't stop after a good night's sleep. It just kept going and going, every single day, and it got louder. \n\nAnd then that feeling started, that awful, heavy feeling that they aren't just saying my name, they are watching every single thing I do. Nine whole months of coming home, locking my deadbolt, checking the windows, and still feeling like eyes are just burning right into the back of my neck. These fixed fears that they're analyzing my work, judging if I turned my patients right or if I spoke to them properly... it's been nine months of this constant, terrifying feeling that I'm being watched and graded by whoever is standing right there in the room with me. Or I'll turn back around and just notice that bright yellow color on the table, and it just pulls me right away. Next thing I know, I’m off doing something else before I even know it. I'll be in the bathroom scrubbing the sink because I saw a yellow sponge, completely forgetting that I was supposed to be writing down Mrs. Miller's blood pressure or finishing that row of yarn. It's just exhausting, doctor, because I start a hundred different things and my mind just gets yanked away by every little noise outside or every color in the room. I just bounce around my little apartment like a pinball all evening and nothing ever actually gets finished.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant changes in your mood, such as feeling unusually low or hopeless, or perhaps feeling extremely energetic and unable to sleep?\n\nPatient: Oh, my mood... well, yes, it's just been all over the place, but not so much the hopeless kind. It’s more like... I’ll go from feeling so bright and chosen, like God Himself has tapped me on the shoulder for something truly grand. I get this buzzing energy in my chest where I just know that Greenville can’t hold all my plans. I'll start thinking about how I'm going to reform the whole caregiving system, how I'm going to open a dozen facilities, and I just feel so big and important, like I could walk right through walls if I wanted to. My mind is just racing with all these magnificent ideas for the future, and I feel so good, so incredibly powerful and ready to take on the whole world. \n\nBut then, almost out of nowhere, that same energy just turns... sour. But... it's not just then. \n\nI remember this one stretch, it was maybe a couple months ago. That big, grand energy just kind of fizzled out, like a balloon losing its air, and I was finally getting some proper rest. Just weeks and weeks of feeling completely normal in my mood, but still having to check the deadbolt and look over my shoulder because I knew they were there, grading me. So, no, they don't just happen when I'm feeling all powerful or mean. They stick around even when I'm just trying to be plain old me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that the voices and the feeling of being watched persisted even when your mood felt completely normal; can you tell me roughly how many weeks in a row that lasted?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back on that to give you a proper number, because I want to be accurate. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I feel like I just... I just said that, didn't I? About the time with the older gentleman on Elm Street? But I suppose you want to know if there were *other* times besides that one. Let me really think on it, because these last nine months have just been such a blur of things happening and shifting. 

But, no, when I really look back at the whole stretch of it... that time I just told you about, that was the main one. That was the specific time, right after my energy finally dropped down and I was sleeping more normally again, getting my full rest. That was when the voices and that terrible, heavy belief that people were tracking my work and judging me just kept going and going for several weeks. Like I said, it was a good three or four weeks of that happening while my mood was perfectly fine and steady. 

I don't recall another stretch quite like that one where the mood was quiet but the rest of it was so loud, at least not for weeks at a time. It's usually all tangled up together, you know? Except for that one period. I remember it so clearly because of how frustrating it was, making his oatmeal and feeling so level-headed, but still having to check the windows. It really sticks out in my mind because I just couldn't understand why the whispers wouldn't stop when I was finally feeling like myself again. So, no, just that one long stretch I was telling you about where it lasted several weeks while my mood was normal.

I was just... going to work, coming home, feeling regular. \n\nBut those voices... Lord, they didn't take a break just because my mood did. They stayed right there. I'd be sleeping fine, feeling calm, and I'd still come home to that whisper. And that awful, heavy feeling that they were in the room, watching me, tracking every little thing I did at work, judging if I rolled a patient right or charted my hours correctly... that kept going on for weeks. Just weeks and weeks of feeling completely normal in my mood, but still having to check the deadbolt and look over my shoulder because I knew they were there, grading me. So, no, they don't just happen when I'm feeling all powerful or mean. They stick around even when I'm just trying to be plain old me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that the voices and the feeling of being watched persisted even when your mood felt completely normal; can you tell me roughly how many weeks in a row that lasted?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back on that to give you a proper number, because I want to be accurate. I remember exactly when it was, because it was right around the time I picked up a new morning shift with this sweet older gentleman over on Elm Street. He mostly just needs help getting dressed and making sure he eats his oatmeal, nothing too heavy. \n\nSo, there was that whole time right after my energy dropped, and I was finally sleeping more normally again. I mean, I was getting my full eight hours, waking up feeling rested, making my coffee, and I wasn't feeling angry or grand or anything like that. Just plain old me. But even with my mood so level, the voices and that awful belief that people were judging or tracking my work... well, it kept going for several weeks. I'd say it was at least three or four solid weeks in a row. \n\nI'd be standing there in my client's kitchen, stirring a little brown sugar into his bowl, feeling perfectly calm in my own heart, but I'd still hear them whispering about me. And I had this heavy, terrible feeling sitting right on my shoulders that the agency had sent folks to watch me from outside the window. I just knew they were tracking my hours, judging every little stir of that spoon, grading whether I was a good enough caregiver. It's just so exhausting, doctor, because you'd think if you're finally sleeping good and your mood is quiet, the rest of it would just pack up and go away too, right? But no, for those several weeks, day in and day out, I'd go to work feeling completely regular in my spirits, but I was still looking over my shoulder, checking the blinds, just waiting for the people who were grading me to show themselves. It just dragged on and on like that for nearly a month before things shifted again...\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the entire nine months, have there been any other periods where the voices and the feeling of being watched lasted for two weeks or more while your mood was completely stable?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I feel like I just... I just said that, didn't I? About the time with the older gentleman on Elm Street? But I suppose you want to know if there were *other* times besides that one. Let me really think on it, because these last nine months have just been such a blur of things happening and shifting. \n\nBut, no, when I really look back at the whole stretch of it... that time I just told you about, that was the main one. It's usually all tangled up together, you know? Except for that one period. I remember it so clearly because of how frustrating it was, making his oatmeal and feeling so level-headed, but still having to check the windows. It really sticks out in my mind because I just couldn't understand why the whispers wouldn't stop when I was finally feeling like myself again. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, see... it's like I was saying before, it's all so tangled up together. When my mood goes off the rails, the rest of it is right there riding shotgun. I don't ever just have the mood swings by themselves, not in these last nine months, anyway. 

Like... well, take that time when I was just buzzing with all that energy, you know? When I was only sleeping maybe two, three hours a night because my brain just wouldn't turn off. I was so wound up with these huge plans, these grand ideas about combining my caregiving with this massive art project I wanted to do. I was going to turn my whole garage into a studio, and I was up all night sketching things out, making lists, just feeling like I was on top of the world and could accomplish anything. I was so sure it was going to be this empire, like I told you. 

But even then, when I was flying that high and feeling so powerful... the voices were right there with me in the studio. I'd be out there at three in the morning, painting or organizing supplies, and I'd hear them. And I was just so certain, I mean absolutely convinced in my bones, that people were watching me work. I felt like there were eyes on me while I was trying to build this big dream of mine. It wasn't just the high mood; it was the voices and the watching, all at the same time. It's never just the energy or just the mood without the other stuff creeping in. They always seem to show up to the party together, except for that one time I told you about when my mood was normal. But if my mood is acting up, you can bet your bottom dollar the voices and the eyes are right there, too.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.0, F25.1, F25.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. Smith. It's nice to meet you. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello Dr. Smith. I... I came in today because things have just been so unsettling lately, and I really didn't know what else to do. I live by myself in this little studio apartment, you know, just a small place, and usually it's very peaceful when I get off work. I'm a personal care aide, so I spend all day on my feet taking care of other folks, and when I get home, I just want quiet. \n\nBut I keep hearing these voices. And I don't mean like the TV is on next door or folks talking out on the street, I mean it sounds as clear as somebody standing right next to me in my little studio. They just keep saying my name, over and over, and telling me that my work is being judged. It's so real, I swear someone is standing right behind me watching me, telling me I'm being judged for what I do and how I'm doing it. But then I turn around to look, and the house is completely quiet. There is absolutely nobody there. I check the locks, I look in the bathroom, I check the closets, and it's just empty, but I still hear them talking to me like they're right in the middle of the room with me. It just doesn't stop, and I... I just can't figure out how they're getting in or where it's coming from when it's just me in there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these voices?\n\nPatient: Oh, Lord, it feels like forever, to be completely honest with you. But... if I really sit down and think back on it, count the weeks and everything, it’s been about nine months now. \n\nI remember because I thought at first it was just me being worn out. You know how it is when you're working so hard and your brain just kind of plays tricks on you? I'm on my feet all day, lifting folks, bathing them, and I'd come home and be washing my dishes or folding my scrubs for the next day, and I'd hear it. Just that whisper. And I'd tell myself, you just need a good night's sleep, you're just exhausted from the shift. But then it didn't stop after a good night's sleep. It just kept going and going, every single day, and it got louder. \n\nAnd then that feeling started, that awful, heavy feeling that they aren't just saying my name, they are watching every single thing I do. Nine whole months of coming home, locking my deadbolt, checking the windows, and still feeling like eyes are just burning right into the back of my neck. These fixed fears that they're analyzing my work, judging if I turned my patients right or if I spoke to them properly... it's been nine months of this constant, terrifying feeling that I'm being watched and graded by whoever is standing right there in the room with me. It's just exhausting, doctor, it really is, because you can't ever truly sit down and rest when you've had that going on in your own home for nine months straight.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the voices, have you noticed any changes in your memory, your ability to focus on tasks, or any confusion about things like the date or where you are?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as knowing where I am or what the date is, I'm fine on that front. I know exactly what day it is and I know I'm right here in your office. But my focus... Lord, my focus is just completely shot to pieces lately. It's like my brain just won't stay put on any one thing for more than a single second. \n\nI'll sit down at my little kitchen table—it's this bright yellow table I picked up at a yard sale a few years back, I thought the color would cheer up the apartment, you know—and I’ll try to finish a care note for the agency. Because we have to write down everything, what the client ate, if they took their pills, all those details they need for the records. Or, if I'm off the clock, I'll try to just sit and do a crochet row. I'm trying to make a little baby blanket for my niece, just a simple pattern. But I just can't stick to it. I'll have the pen in my hand or the yarn looped over my finger, and then... I don't know, a car door will slam outside on the street, and suddenly I'm up out of my chair looking out the glass to see who's parking. \n\nAnd then maybe there's a bird at the window, just a little sparrow or something picking at the sill, and I'm just standing there watching that bird instead of doing my work. Or I'll turn back around and just notice that bright yellow color on the table, and it just pulls me right away. Next thing I know, I’m off doing something else before I even know it. I'll be in the bathroom scrubbing the sink because I saw a yellow sponge, completely forgetting that I was supposed to be writing down Mrs. Miller's blood pressure or finishing that row of yarn. It's just exhausting, doctor, because I start a hundred different things and my mind just gets yanked away by every little noise outside or every color in the room. I just bounce around my little apartment like a pinball all evening and nothing ever actually gets finished.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant changes in your mood, such as feeling unusually low or hopeless, or perhaps feeling extremely energetic and unable to sleep?\n\nPatient: Oh, my mood... well, yes, it's just been all over the place, but not so much the hopeless kind. It’s more like... I’ll go from feeling so bright and chosen, like God Himself has tapped me on the shoulder for something truly grand. I get this buzzing energy in my chest where I just know that Greenville can’t hold all my plans. I'll start thinking about how I'm going to reform the whole caregiving system, how I'm going to open a dozen facilities, and I just feel so big and important, like I could walk right through walls if I wanted to. My mind is just racing with all these magnificent ideas for the future, and I feel so good, so incredibly powerful and ready to take on the whole world. \n\nBut then, almost out of nowhere, that same energy just turns... sour. It gets prickly and hot. I'll go right from feeling like I'm on top of the world to snapping hard at someone over a tiny, insignificant little thing. Like the other day at work, I was helping one of my regular clients, and he just didn't hear me right. He just asked me to repeat myself about what we were having for lunch. Just a simple, harmless little question, right? And I just snapped at him. I mean, I bit his head off over absolutely nothing, telling him I don't have time to say things twice. I felt awful about it later, because that's not me, I'm a patient person, I have to be for this job. You can't be mean to folks who need your help. But in that moment, my skin was just crawling with irritation. It’s just this wild swing from feeling like the most special, capable woman in all of Greenville to being so mean and irritable over a simple question, and I just can't seem to stop the train once it gets going like that.\n\nDoctor: When you have these periods of feeling powerful and grand, or when you feel irritable and mean, do the voices and the feeling of being watched happen at the same time, or do they also happen when your mood is completely normal?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing that really gets me tangled up when I try to make sense of it all. Because, yes, when I'm buzzing high and feeling like I'm going to build that whole caregiving empire, they're there. And when I'm irritable and snapping at folks, they're there. But... it's not just then. \n\nI remember this one stretch, it was maybe a couple months ago. That big, grand energy just kind of fizzled out, like a balloon losing its air, and I was finally getting some proper rest. I was sleeping through the night, getting my solid eight hours, waking up feeling physically normal, you know? My mood wasn't flying high, and I wasn't snapping at anybody. I was just... going to work, coming home, feeling regular. \n\nBut those voices... Lord, they didn't take a break just because my mood did. They stayed right there. I'd be sleeping fine, feeling calm, and I'd still come home to that whisper. And that awful, heavy feeling that they were in the room, watching me, tracking every little thing I did at work, judging if I rolled a patient right or charted my hours correctly... that kept going on for weeks. Just weeks and weeks of feeling completely normal in my mood, but still having to check the deadbolt and look over my shoulder because I knew they were there, grading me. So, no, they don't just happen when I'm feeling all powerful or mean. They stick around even when I'm just trying to be plain old me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that the voices and the feeling of being watched persisted even when your mood felt completely normal; can you tell me roughly how many weeks in a row that lasted?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back on that to give you a proper number, because I want to be accurate. I remember exactly when it was, because it was right around the time I picked up a new morning shift with this sweet older gentleman over on Elm Street. He mostly just needs help getting dressed and making sure he eats his oatmeal, nothing too heavy. \n\nSo, there was that whole time right after my energy dropped, and I was finally sleeping more normally again. I mean, I was getting my full eight hours, waking up feeling rested, making my coffee, and I wasn't feeling angry or grand or anything like that. Just plain old me. But even with my mood so level, the voices and that awful belief that people were judging or tracking my work... well, it kept going for several weeks. I'd say it was at least three or four solid weeks in a row. \n\nI'd be standing there in my client's kitchen, stirring a little brown sugar into his bowl, feeling perfectly calm in my own heart, but I'd still hear them whispering about me. And I had this heavy, terrible feeling sitting right on my shoulders that the agency had sent folks to watch me from outside the window. I just knew they were tracking my hours, judging every little stir of that spoon, grading whether I was a good enough caregiver. It's just so exhausting, doctor, because you'd think if you're finally sleeping good and your mood is quiet, the rest of it would just pack up and go away too, right? But no, for those several weeks, day in and day out, I'd go to work feeling completely regular in my spirits, but I was still looking over my shoulder, checking the blinds, just waiting for the people who were grading me to show themselves. It just dragged on and on like that for nearly a month before things shifted again...\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the entire nine months, have there been any other periods where the voices and the feeling of being watched lasted for two weeks or more while your mood was completely stable?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I feel like I just... I just said that, didn't I? About the time with the older gentleman on Elm Street? But I suppose you want to know if there were *other* times besides that one. Let me really think on it, because these last nine months have just been such a blur of things happening and shifting. \n\nBut, no, when I really look back at the whole stretch of it... that time I just told you about, that was the main one. That was the specific time, right after my energy finally dropped down and I was sleeping more normally again, getting my full rest. That was when the voices and that terrible, heavy belief that people were tracking my work and judging me just kept going and going for several weeks. Like I said, it was a good three or four weeks of that happening while my mood was perfectly fine and steady. \n\nI don't recall another stretch quite like that one where the mood was quiet but the rest of it was so loud, at least not for weeks at a time. It's usually all tangled up together, you know? Except for that one period. I remember it so clearly because of how frustrating it was, making his oatmeal and feeling so level-headed, but still having to check the windows. It really sticks out in my mind because I just couldn't understand why the whispers wouldn't stop when I was finally feeling like myself again. So, no, just that one long stretch I was telling you about where it lasted several weeks while my mood was normal.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the times when you were hearing voices and feeling watched, have there been any other periods during these past nine months where you experienced those intense mood swings—feeling either very high and energetic or very low and hopeless—without any voices or feelings of being watched at all?\n\nPatient: Well, no, see... it's like I was saying before, it's all so tangled up together. When my mood goes off the rails, the rest of it is right there riding shotgun. I don't ever just have the mood swings by themselves, not in these last nine months, anyway. \n\nLike... well, take that time when I was just buzzing with all that energy, you know? When I was only sleeping maybe two, three hours a night because my brain just wouldn't turn off. I was so wound up with these huge plans, these grand ideas about combining my caregiving with this massive art project I wanted to do. I was going to turn my whole garage into a studio, and I was up all night sketching things out, making lists, just feeling like I was on top of the world and could accomplish anything. I was so sure it was going to be this empire, like I told you. \n\nBut even then, when I was flying that high and feeling so powerful... the voices were right there with me in the studio. I'd be out there at three in the morning, painting or organizing supplies, and I'd hear them. And I was just so certain, I mean absolutely convinced in my bones, that people were watching me work. I felt like there were eyes on me while I was trying to build this big dream of mine. It wasn't just the high mood; it was the voices and the watching, all at the same time. It's never just the energy or just the mood without the other stuff creeping in. They always seem to show up to the party together, except for that one time I told you about when my mood was normal. Well, no... no, I haven't really had anything quite like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about, that real deep, heavy sadness where you just can't get out of bed and everything feels like it's covered in a thick gray blanket. I've seen folks go through that, especially in my line of work. You see a lot of lonely older people who just lose their spark, you know? They stop wanting to eat, stop wanting to watch their favorite shows, just sit there staring out the window. It breaks your heart to see it. 

But for me, in these last nine months? No, I haven't had a stretch like that, not for two weeks or anything close to it. It's been more of the... the other way around, like I was telling you with the garage and the art project and all that buzzing energy. That's been the real struggle. I haven't had a time where I just lost interest in everything or felt completely hopeless and sad for weeks on end. It's just been so much of the high energy, the fast thoughts, and then the voices and the feeling of being watched all mixed in with it. I mean, sure, I get frustrated, and it's exhausting dealing with the whispers and the feeling like I'm being graded all the time, but it's not that deep, empty sadness you're asking about. I still care about my clients, I still want to do my job, even when it's hard. So, no, I can't say I've had a period of being deeply sad or losing interest like that. It's just not been part of the picture for me lately.

Well, see, doctor, it's just... I feel like we keep going round and round about this one specific time, don't we? Well, let me think on that... looking way, way back, even before I started working with the current agency. You know, back when I was doing private care over in the valley, which was... oh, Lord, that must have been twenty years ago now. No. No, I can tell you for a fact, it's never been like that. I had it all mapped out in my head, felt like an absolute genius. 

But even then, even way back then, I never got to just enjoy the high. The voices were right there in the living room with me. I'd be scrubbing away at four in the morning, and I'd hear them whispering about how I was doing it wrong, and I'd get that awful, heavy feeling that someone was standing right outside my window watching me work. I'd have to keep the blinds pulled tight, but I could still feel the eyes on me. 

So, no, they’re always attached at the hip. If my motor is running that fast and I'm feeling that big and grand, you can be absolutely sure the passengers are already in the car, talking about me and watching my every move. I’ve never had a week or even a few days of just the energy and the grand ideas without the rest of that mess coming along to ruin it. It just doesn't happen that way for me.
